[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n13\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF90.0, F90.8, F90.9\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I’m here because I just… I can’t seem to keep my mind on one thing anymore, and it’s gotten to where it’s really messing up my days. I’m a carpenter, so I’m used to noise and people moving around, that’s not new, but lately it’s like one saw starting up, or somebody walking past the office, or even just me remembering some other job I’m supposed to check on, and I’m gone. My attention just gets pulled right off what I was doing.\n\nAnd then I’ll come back to it and realize I’ve lost a chunk of time, or I’m halfway into something else, and the whole day kind of slips away from me. It feels like I’m constantly being yanked from one thing to another, even when I’m trying hard to stay put mentally. That’s really what brought me in.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself fidgeting or moving around more than usual when you're trying to focus on a task?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. It’s not always like I’m tapping my foot every second or anything, but when I have to sit still and focus, especially at the desk doing estimates, I feel like I’m all wound up inside. Like my body is sitting there, but the rest of me is saying, get up, go check something, walk around, do anything else.\n\nLong meetings are bad for that too. I’ll be sitting there trying to listen and stay put, and I just get this uncomfortable need to move around. And the thing is, my wrist is healed now, so it’s not like I have some physical reason I can point to where I need to keep adjusting or getting up. I know I’m supposed to just sit there and finish what I’m doing, but it feels like I’m fighting myself the whole time.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking things through?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure I’d say that’s a big thing for me. I mean, everybody does something without thinking once in a while, I suppose, but I don’t think of myself as someone who’s constantly rushing into decisions or doing wild things on a whim.\n\nIf anything, I can get stuck turning something over in my head too much, especially if it’s important. So… no, I haven’t really noticed acting impulsively as one of the main problems. I’d have to think about what kinds of things you mean, though.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble keeping your workspace or tasks organized?\n\nPatient: Yes. That’s actually become one of the worst parts of it, especially since I’ve been stuck doing more of the shop paperwork. Out on a job site, things have a kind of order to them because the work is right in front of me, you know? But with paperwork, I’ll have bids that need finishing, schedules that need changing, invoices sitting there, and then an apprentice comes over with a question, and somehow it all just lands in one big pile on my bench.\n\nI’ll look at it and I honestly can’t always figure out what needs doing first. It’s not that I don’t care or I don’t know the work — I do — but deciding what to start with, what can wait, what has to go out today, it just gets tangled up. So instead of moving through it in order, I’ll start one thing, remember another thing, shift papers around, answer half a question, then suddenly I’m rushing at the end of the day and still missing deadlines. It’s embarrassing, because I feel like I should be able to keep bids, schedules, invoices, all of that straight, but lately it just piles up faster than I can sort it.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your mood lately?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’ve noticed a clear change in my mood, exactly. I mean, I get frustrated, sure, but I think anybody would when they’re trying to get things done and it feels like the day keeps slipping away from them. But as far as feeling like my mood has changed in some big way, I’m not sure I’d say that.\n\nI haven’t really been tracking it, if that makes sense. I don’t wake up and think, oh, I’m in a different mood than usual. It’s more like I notice how I’m doing based on how the day is going, and if things are backed up or I’m not keeping up, then I can get irritated with myself. But I don’t know if that counts as mood or just being aggravated by the situation.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself talking more than others in social situations?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I don’t think I’m usually the person taking over a whole conversation, if that’s what you mean. I mean, with people I know, I can talk plenty, especially if it’s about work or something practical, but I don’t think others would say I’m always talking more than everyone else.\n\nSometimes I might go on a bit if I’m trying to explain something, because I want to make sure I’m saying it right, and then I realize I’ve added more detail than maybe they needed. But socially, like at a gathering or with a group, I don’t see myself as especially talkative. If anything, I’m often listening or waiting for the right time to jump in. So… no, I haven’t really noticed that as a regular problem.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or feel the need to get up and move around when you're in quiet or low-stimulation environments?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. That’s actually one of the things that makes the desk work so hard for me. If I’m sitting there doing estimates, or if I’m stuck in a long meeting where everybody’s just talking through details, I start feeling really wound up inside. It’s not always like I’m jumping out of my chair right away, but inside I’m sort of crawling, like my body wants me to get up and move around.\n\nAnd the thing is, I know I’m supposed to just stay put and finish. My wrist is healed now, so it’s not like I have that excuse anymore where I’m limited physically or need to be careful in the same way. I’ll tell myself, “Just sit here, get through the estimates, finish this one thing,” but after a while it feels almost uncomfortable to keep sitting there. I’ll want to stand up, walk across the shop, check something, move papers around, anything besides just sitting quietly and staying on that one task.\n\nMeetings are the same way. If it’s short and to the point, I can manage better, but if it drags on, I feel like I’m holding myself in place. I’m trying to listen and look like I’m listening, but inside I’m thinking, I need to get up, I need to move, I need to do something with my hands. It’s frustrating because I’m not trying to be rude or impatient, but sitting still in that kind of quiet, slow situation feels a lot harder than it probably should.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find that unrelated thoughts keep popping into your mind when you're trying to focus?\n\nPatient: Yes. That’s probably one of the biggest reasons I’m here, honestly. I’ll be trying to do one thing—like sit down in the office and work through an estimate or figure out materials for a job—and it’s like my mind will not stay on that one track. It doesn’t take much at all.\n\nA saw starts up out in the shop, or somebody walks past the office door, or I hear a truck pull in, and suddenly my attention is gone. And sometimes it’s not even something outside me. I’ll just have a thought pop in about another job, like, “Oh, did we order that trim?” or “I need to call that customer back,” and then I’m off into that, and the thing I was doing is just sitting there half-finished.\n\nThe frustrating part is I don’t always notice right away. I’ll think I’m still working, but then I look up and I’ve spent twenty minutes bouncing between three different things, and the estimate I sat down to finish is still not done. Then the whole day starts getting away from me, because one interruption turns into another, and by the end I feel like I’ve been busy all day but didn’t actually keep my mind on what needed doing.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it difficult to dismiss thoughts that aren't relevant to what you're doing?\n\nPatient: Yes, very much. That’s really the part that wears me out, because once a thought gets in there, it’s not like I can just say, “That doesn’t matter right now,” and put it aside. I’ll be trying to keep my mind on one thing, like an estimate or some paperwork, and then one saw starts up, or somebody walks past the office, or I remember something from another job, and that’s it — I’m pulled off.\n\nAnd I’ll try to bring myself back, I really will. I’ll think, “No, finish what you’re doing first,” but the other thought keeps tugging at me. Like if I suddenly think, “Did we order that material?” then I feel like I have to check it right then, because otherwise I’m afraid I’ll forget. But then while I’m checking that, something else comes up, and before I know it I’m not doing the original thing at all.\n\nThat’s honestly what brings me in. I feel like I cannot keep my mind on one thing anymore. It’s not just being a little distracted here and there — it’s like the whole day can get away from me from one small thing pulling me off track.\n\nDoctor: Did you have a lot of energy or difficulty sitting still when you were a child?\n\nPatient: I don’t remember myself as being wild exactly, like running all over the classroom or anything, but yes, there were definitely problems even when I was little. Back in grade school, before I was 12, the comments my mother got from teachers were always kind of the same: that I was bright, but careless. That word came up a lot.\n\nThey’d say I could understand the work, but then I’d lose the homework, or forget what the instructions were, or I’d be sitting there staring out the window instead of finishing whatever seatwork we were supposed to be doing. I can still picture that, honestly — everybody else bent over their papers, and I’d be looking outside or thinking about something else, and then suddenly realize I hadn’t gotten much done.\n\nSo I don’t know if I’d describe it as “a lot of energy” so much as I had a hard time staying with what I was supposed to be doing while sitting still in class. It was enough that the teachers kept bringing it up to my mother.\n\nDoctor: Were you often described as fidgety or restless as a child?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I was described that way as a child, honestly. I don’t really remember teachers saying “fidgety” or “restless” specifically.\n\nWhat I can say is that now, if I have to sit at the desk doing estimates, or I’m stuck sitting through a long meeting, I get this wound-up feeling inside. It’s like my body is sitting there, but everything in me wants to get up and move around. And I know there’s no good reason for it — my wrist is healed, I’m physically able to just sit there and finish the work — but I feel this pressure, like I need to stand up, go check something, walk out to the shop, anything except keep sitting still.\n\nIt’s not always that I’m bouncing around in an obvious way. It’s more like I’m uncomfortable in my own skin when I’m forced to stay put. I’ll tell myself, “Just finish this estimate first,” but the longer I sit there, the more restless I feel.\n\nDoctor: Did you experience similar difficulties with focus and restlessness as a child, or did these issues start more recently?\n\nPatient: No, it didn’t start recently. It’s gotten more noticeable now, I think, because my work has more paperwork and estimates and things I have to sit and focus on, but when I really think back, the focus part was there when I was a kid.\n\nEven in grade school, before I was 12, my teachers were already telling my mother the same kinds of things. They’d say I was bright, I could understand the material, but I was careless. I remember that word because it felt like everybody thought I just wasn’t trying hard enough. I’d lose homework, or I’d forget what the instructions were supposed to be, or I’d start seatwork and then end up staring out the window while everyone else was finishing.\n\nSo, no, it doesn’t feel like this came out of nowhere later in life. The restlessness maybe I notice differently now, but the trouble staying focused and following through was there pretty early.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed your difficulties with focus and restlessness affecting your relationships with family, friends, or coworkers?\n\nPatient: Yes. Unfortunately, yes. I wish I could say it was just annoying to me, but it affects other people too.\n\nAt work it’s been a real problem. I’m a carpenter, and I’m supposed to be dependable with estimates, ordering materials, scheduling things, keeping track of what needs to happen next. And when I miss something or delay something, it doesn’t just stay on paper — it turns into somebody showing up and the materials aren’t there, or I forgot to order something, or I made a mistake and then we have to redo part of a job. That costs time, and sometimes it costs me commissions too, because if the estimate is late or wrong, or I don’t follow through fast enough, the work goes somewhere else or the job gets messed up.\n\nAnd I can tell the crew doesn’t rely on me the same way anymore for certain things. Nobody’s cruel about it, but I notice it. They’ll double-check me, or they’ll ask someone else about the schedule, or they don’t want me to be the one responsible for ordering because they’re afraid something will get missed. That feels awful, because I know my trade. I’m not lazy, and I’m not trying to dump work on anyone, but my mistakes make it look that way sometimes.\n\nAt home, it’s affected my husband too. He’s had to take over the bills and a lot of the reminders because if papers come into the house, I’ll set them somewhere “safe” and then they’re gone, or I’ll miss what needs to be done until it’s already a problem. He’ll remind me about things more than once, and I get embarrassed and defensive sometimes, even though he’s usually just trying to keep things from falling through the cracks.\n\nSo yes, it’s definitely caused strain. It’s not just in my head — other people end up having to work around me.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

I’ll just have a thought pop in about another job, like, “Oh, did we order that trim?” or “I need to call that customer back,” and then I’m off into that, and the thing I was doing is just sitting there half-finished.\n\nThe frustrating part is I don’t always notice right away. I’ll think I’m still working, but then I look up and I’ve spent twenty minutes bouncing between three different things, and the estimate I sat down to finish is still not done. Then the whole day starts getting away from me, because one interruption turns into another, and by the end I feel like I’ve been busy all day but didn’t actually keep my mind on what needed doing.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it difficult to dismiss thoughts that aren't relevant to what you're doing?\n\nPatient: Yes, very much. That’s really the part that wears me out, because once a thought gets in there, it’s not like I can just say, “That doesn’t matter right now,” and put it aside. I’ll be trying to keep my mind on one thing, like an estimate or some paperwork, and then one saw starts up, or somebody walks past the office, or I remember something from another job, and that’s it — I’m pulled off.\n\nAnd I’ll try to bring myself back, I really will. I’ll think, “No, finish what you’re doing first,” but the other thought keeps tugging at me. Like if I suddenly think, “Did we order that material?” then I feel like I have to check it right then, because otherwise I’m afraid I’ll forget. But then while I’m checking that, something else comes up, and before I know it I’m not doing the original thing at all.\n\nThat’s honestly what brings me in. I feel like I cannot keep my mind on one thing anymore. It’s not just being a little distracted here and there — it’s like the whole day can get away from me from one small thing pulling me off track.\n\nDoctor: Did you have a lot of energy or difficulty sitting still when you were a child?\n\nPatient: I don’t remember myself as being wild exactly, like running all over the classroom or anything, but yes, there were definitely problems even when I was little. Back in grade school, before I was 12, the comments my mother got from teachers were always kind of the same: that I was bright, but careless. That word came up a lot.\n\nThey’d say I could understand the work, but then I’d lose the homework, or forget what the instructions were, or I’d be sitting there staring out the window instead of finishing whatever seatwork we were supposed to be doing. I can still picture that, honestly — everybody else bent over their papers, and I’d be looking outside or thinking about something else, and then suddenly realize I hadn’t gotten much done.\n\nSo I don’t know if I’d describe it as “a lot of energy” so much as I had a hard time staying with what I was supposed to be doing while sitting still in class. It was enough that the teachers kept bringing it up to my mother.\n\nDoctor: Were you often described as fidgety or restless as a child?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I was described that way as a child, honestly. I don’t really remember teachers saying “fidgety” or “restless” specifically.\n\nWhat I can say is that now, if I have to sit at the desk doing estimates, or I’m stuck sitting through a long meeting, I get this wound-up feeling inside. It’s like my body is sitting there, but everything in me wants to get up and move around. And I know there’s no good reason for it — my wrist is healed, I’m physically able to just sit there and finish the work — but I feel this pressure, like I need to stand up, go check something, walk out to the shop, anything except keep sitting still.\n\nIt’s not always that I’m bouncing around in an obvious way. It’s more like I’m uncomfortable in my own skin when I’m forced to stay put. I’ll tell myself, “Just finish this estimate first,” but the longer I sit there, the more restless I feel.\n\nDoctor: Did you experience similar difficulties with focus and restlessness as a child, or did these issues start more recently?\n\nPatient: No, it didn’t start recently. It’s gotten more noticeable now, I think, because my work has more paperwork and estimates and things I have to sit and focus on, but when I really think back, the focus part was there when I was a kid.\n\nEven in grade school, before I was 12, my teachers were already telling my mother the same kinds of things. They’d say I was bright, I could understand the material, but I was careless. I remember that word because it felt like everybody thought I just wasn’t trying hard enough. I’d lose homework, or I’d forget what the instructions were supposed to be, or I’d start seatwork and then end up staring out the window while everyone else was finishing.\n\nSo, no, it doesn’t feel like this came out of nowhere later in life. The restlessness maybe I notice differently now, but the trouble staying focused and following through was there pretty early.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed your difficulties with focus and restlessness affecting your relationships with family, friends, or coworkers?\n\nPatient: Yes. Unfortunately, yes. I wish I could say it was just annoying to me, but it affects other people too.\n\nAt work it’s been a real problem. I’m a carpenter, and I’m supposed to be dependable with estimates, ordering materials, scheduling things, keeping track of what needs to happen next. And when I miss something or delay something, it doesn’t just stay on paper — it turns into somebody showing up and the materials aren’t there, or I forgot to order something, or I made a mistake and then we have to redo part of a job. That costs time, and sometimes it costs me commissions too, because if the estimate is late or wrong, or I don’t follow through fast enough, the work goes somewhere else or the job gets messed up.\n\nAnd I can tell the crew doesn’t rely on me the same way anymore for certain things. Nobody’s cruel about it, but I notice it. They’ll double-check me, or they’ll ask someone else about the schedule, or they don’t want me to be the one responsible for ordering because they’re afraid something will get missed. That feels awful, because I know my trade. I’m not lazy, and I’m not trying to dump work on anyone, but my mistakes make it look that way sometimes.\n\nAt home, it’s affected my husband too. He’s had to take over the bills and a lot of the reminders because if papers come into the house, I’ll set them somewhere “safe” and then they’re gone, or I’ll miss what needs to be done until it’s already a problem. He’ll remind me about things more than once, and I get embarrassed and defensive sometimes, even though he’s usually just trying to keep things from falling through the cracks.\n\nSo yes, it’s definitely caused strain. It’s not just in my head — other people end up having to work around me.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes. I mean… very much so. It’s hard to say that without feeling like I’m making excuses, but yes, it has affected my life in a pretty serious way.

At work, the mistakes and delays aren’t small things anymore. If I’m late getting an estimate done, or I forget to order something, or I don’t keep the schedule straight, it can throw off the whole job. Then materials aren’t there when they’re supposed to be, or something has to be redone, or we lose time trying to fix what should have been handled earlier. And that has cost me commissions, too. It’s not just embarrassing — it hits me financially.

And I can feel that the crew doesn’t trust me the same way with certain responsibilities now. They’ll double-check me, or they’d rather someone else handle ordering or scheduling because they’re worried I’ll miss something. That really bothers me, because I know I’m capable in the actual carpentry. I know the work. But these parts of the job make me look unreliable.

At home, my husband has had to take over the bills and reminders because if I’m the one handling papers, I’ll put them somewhere and then they’re just… gone. Or I’ll miss what needs to be done until it’s overdue or causing stress. He ends up carrying that mental load, and I hate that. So yes, overall, it’s affected my work, my money, my confidence, and my marriage. It’s not just an inconvenience.

[
  {

    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n14\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF90.0, F90.8, F90.9\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I’m here because I just… I can’t seem to keep my mind on one thing anymore, and it’s gotten to where it’s really messing up my days. I’m a carpenter, so I’m used to noise and people moving around, that’s not new, but lately it’s like one saw starting up, or somebody walking past the office, or even just me remembering some other job I’m supposed to check on, and I’m gone. My attention just gets pulled right off what I was doing.\n\nAnd then I’ll come back to it and realize I’ve lost a chunk of time, or I’m halfway into something else, and the whole day kind of slips away from me. It feels like I’m constantly being yanked from one thing to another, even when I’m trying hard to stay put mentally. That’s really what brought me in.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself fidgeting or moving around more than usual when you're trying to focus on a task?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. It’s not always like I’m tapping my foot every second or anything, but when I have to sit still and focus, especially at the desk doing estimates, I feel like I’m all wound up inside. Like my body is sitting there, but the rest of me is saying, get up, go check something, walk around, do anything else.\n\nLong meetings are bad for that too. I’ll be sitting there trying to listen and stay put, and I just get this uncomfortable need to move around. And the thing is, my wrist is healed now, so it’s not like I have some physical reason I can point to where I need to keep adjusting or getting up. I know I’m supposed to just sit there and finish what I’m doing, but it feels like I’m fighting myself the whole time.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking things through?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure I’d say that’s a big thing for me. I mean, everybody does something without thinking once in a while, I suppose, but I don’t think of myself as someone who’s constantly rushing into decisions or doing wild things on a whim.\n\nIf anything, I can get stuck turning something over in my head too much, especially if it’s important. So… no, I haven’t really noticed acting impulsively as one of the main problems. I’d have to think about what kinds of things you mean, though.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble keeping your workspace or tasks organized?\n\nPatient: Yes. That’s actually become one of the worst parts of it, especially since I’ve been stuck doing more of the shop paperwork. Out on a job site, things have a kind of order to them because the work is right in front of me, you know? But with paperwork, I’ll have bids that need finishing, schedules that need changing, invoices sitting there, and then an apprentice comes over with a question, and somehow it all just lands in one big pile on my bench.\n\nI’ll look at it and I honestly can’t always figure out what needs doing first. It’s not that I don’t care or I don’t know the work — I do — but deciding what to start with, what can wait, what has to go out today, it just gets tangled up. So instead of moving through it in order, I’ll start one thing, remember another thing, shift papers around, answer half a question, then suddenly I’m rushing at the end of the day and still missing deadlines. It’s embarrassing, because I feel like I should be able to keep bids, schedules, invoices, all of that straight, but lately it just piles up faster than I can sort it.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your mood lately?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’ve noticed a clear change in my mood, exactly. I mean, I get frustrated, sure, but I think anybody would when they’re trying to get things done and it feels like the day keeps slipping away from them. But as far as feeling like my mood has changed in some big way, I’m not sure I’d say that.\n\nI haven’t really been tracking it, if that makes sense. I don’t wake up and think, oh, I’m in a different mood than usual. It’s more like I notice how I’m doing based on how the day is going, and if things are backed up or I’m not keeping up, then I can get irritated with myself. But I don’t know if that counts as mood or just being aggravated by the situation.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself talking more than others in social situations?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I don’t think I’m usually the person taking over a whole conversation, if that’s what you mean. I mean, with people I know, I can talk plenty, especially if it’s about work or something practical, but I don’t think others would say I’m always talking more than everyone else.\n\nSometimes I might go on a bit if I’m trying to explain something, because I want to make sure I’m saying it right, and then I realize I’ve added more detail than maybe they needed. But socially, like at a gathering or with a group, I don’t see myself as especially talkative. If anything, I’m often listening or waiting for the right time to jump in. So… no, I haven’t really noticed that as a regular problem.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or feel the need to get up and move around when you're in quiet or low-stimulation environments?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. That’s actually one of the things that makes the desk work so hard for me. If I’m sitting there doing estimates, or if I’m stuck in a long meeting where everybody’s just talking through details, I start feeling really wound up inside. It’s not always like I’m jumping out of my chair right away, but inside I’m sort of crawling, like my body wants me to get up and move around.\n\nAnd the thing is, I know I’m supposed to just stay put and finish. My wrist is healed now, so it’s not like I have that excuse anymore where I’m limited physically or need to be careful in the same way. I’ll tell myself, “Just sit here, get through the estimates, finish this one thing,” but after a while it feels almost uncomfortable to keep sitting there. I’ll want to stand up, walk across the shop, check something, move papers around, anything besides just sitting quietly and staying on that one task.\n\nMeetings are the same way. If it’s short and to the point, I can manage better, but if it drags on, I feel like I’m holding myself in place. I’m trying to listen and look like I’m listening, but inside I’m thinking, I need to get up, I need to move, I need to do something with my hands. It’s frustrating because I’m not trying to be rude or impatient, but sitting still in that kind of quiet, slow situation feels a lot harder than it probably should.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find that unrelated thoughts keep popping into your mind when you're trying to focus?\n\nPatient: Yes. That’s probably one of the biggest reasons I’m here, honestly. I’ll be trying to do one thing—like sit down in the office and work through an estimate or figure out materials for a job—and it’s like my mind will not stay on that one track. It doesn’t take much at all.\n\nA saw starts up out in the shop, or somebody walks past the office door, or I hear a truck pull in, and suddenly my attention is gone. And sometimes it’s not even something outside me. I’ll just have a thought pop in about another job, like, “Oh, did we order that trim?” or “I need to call that customer back,” and then I’m off into that, and the thing I was doing is just sitting there half-finished.\n\nThe frustrating part is I don’t always notice right away. I’ll think I’m still working, but then I look up and I’ve spent twenty minutes bouncing between three different things, and the estimate I sat down to finish is still not done. Then the whole day starts getting away from me, because one interruption turns into another, and by the end I feel like I’ve been busy all day but didn’t actually keep my mind on what needed doing.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it difficult to dismiss thoughts that aren't relevant to what you're doing?\n\nPatient: Yes, very much. That’s really the part that wears me out, because once a thought gets in there, it’s not like I can just say, “That doesn’t matter right now,” and put it aside. I’ll be trying to keep my mind on one thing, like an estimate or some paperwork, and then one saw starts up, or somebody walks past the office, or I remember something from another job, and that’s it — I’m pulled off.\n\nAnd I’ll try to bring myself back, I really will. I’ll think, “No, finish what you’re doing first,” but the other thought keeps tugging at me. Like if I suddenly think, “Did we order that material?” then I feel like I have to check it right then, because otherwise I’m afraid I’ll forget. But then while I’m checking that, something else comes up, and before I know it I’m not doing the original thing at all.\n\nThat’s honestly what brings me in. I feel like I cannot keep my mind on one thing anymore. It’s not just being a little distracted here and there — it’s like the whole day can get away from me from one small thing pulling me off track.\n\nDoctor: Did you have a lot of energy or difficulty sitting still when you were a child?\n\nPatient: I don’t remember myself as being wild exactly, like running all over the classroom or anything, but yes, there were definitely problems even when I was little. Back in grade school, before I was 12, the comments my mother got from teachers were always kind of the same: that I was bright, but careless. That word came up a lot.\n\nThey’d say I could understand the work, but then I’d lose the homework, or forget what the instructions were, or I’d be sitting there staring out the window instead of finishing whatever seatwork we were supposed to be doing. I can still picture that, honestly — everybody else bent over their papers, and I’d be looking outside or thinking about something else, and then suddenly realize I hadn’t gotten much done.\n\nSo I don’t know if I’d describe it as “a lot of energy” so much as I had a hard time staying with what I was supposed to be doing while sitting still in class. It was enough that the teachers kept bringing it up to my mother.\n\nDoctor: Were you often described as fidgety or restless as a child?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I was described that way as a child, honestly. I don’t really remember teachers saying “fidgety” or “restless” specifically.\n\nWhat I can say is that now, if I have to sit at the desk doing estimates, or I’m stuck sitting through a long meeting, I get this wound-up feeling inside. It’s like my body is sitting there, but everything in me wants to get up and move around. And I know there’s no good reason for it — my wrist is healed, I’m physically able to just sit there and finish the work — but I feel this pressure, like I need to stand up, go check something, walk out to the shop, anything except keep sitting still.\n\nIt’s not always that I’m bouncing around in an obvious way. It’s more like I’m uncomfortable in my own skin when I’m forced to stay put. I’ll tell myself, “Just finish this estimate first,” but the longer I sit there, the more restless I feel.\n\nDoctor: Did you experience similar difficulties with focus and restlessness as a child, or did these issues start more recently?\n\nPatient: No, it didn’t start recently. It’s gotten more noticeable now, I think, because my work has more paperwork and estimates and things I have to sit and focus on, but when I really think back, the focus part was there when I was a kid.\n\nEven in grade school, before I was 12, my teachers were already telling my mother the same kinds of things. They’d say I was bright, I could understand the material, but I was careless. I remember that word because it felt like everybody thought I just wasn’t trying hard enough. I’d lose homework, or I’d forget what the instructions were supposed to be, or I’d start seatwork and then end up staring out the window while everyone else was finishing.\n\nSo, no, it doesn’t feel like this came out of nowhere later in life. The restlessness maybe I notice differently now, but the trouble staying focused and following through was there pretty early.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed your difficulties with focus and restlessness affecting your relationships with family, friends, or coworkers?\n\nPatient: Yes. Unfortunately, yes. I wish I could say it was just annoying to me, but it affects other people too.\n\nAt work it’s been a real problem. I’m a carpenter, and I’m supposed to be dependable with estimates, ordering materials, scheduling things, keeping track of what needs to happen next. And when I miss something or delay something, it doesn’t just stay on paper — it turns into somebody showing up and the materials aren’t there, or I forgot to order something, or I made a mistake and then we have to redo part of a job. That costs time, and sometimes it costs me commissions too, because if the estimate is late or wrong, or I don’t follow through fast enough, the work goes somewhere else or the job gets messed up.\n\nAnd I can tell the crew doesn’t rely on me the same way anymore for certain things. Nobody’s cruel about it, but I notice it. They’ll double-check me, or they’ll ask someone else about the schedule, or they don’t want me to be the one responsible for ordering because they’re afraid something will get missed. That feels awful, because I know my trade. I’m not lazy, and I’m not trying to dump work on anyone, but my mistakes make it look that way sometimes.\n\nAt home, it’s affected my husband too. He’s had to take over the bills and a lot of the reminders because if papers come into the house, I’ll set them somewhere “safe” and then they’re gone, or I’ll miss what needs to be done until it’s already a problem. He’ll remind me about things more than once, and I get embarrassed and defensive sometimes, even though he’s usually just trying to keep things from falling through the cracks.\n\nSo yes, it’s definitely caused strain. It’s not just in my head — other people end up having to work around me.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt like your difficulties with focus and restlessness have significantly impacted your overall quality of life?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean… very much so. It’s hard to say that without feeling like I’m making excuses, but yes, it has affected my life in a pretty serious way.\n\nAt work, the mistakes and delays aren’t small things anymore. If I’m late getting an estimate done, or I forget to order something, or I don’t keep the schedule straight, it can throw off the whole job. Then materials aren’t there when they’re supposed to be, or something has to be redone, or we lose time trying to fix what should have been handled earlier. And that has cost me commissions, too. It’s not just embarrassing — it hits me financially.\n\nAnd I can feel that the crew doesn’t trust me the same way with certain responsibilities now. They’ll double-check me, or they’d rather someone else handle ordering or scheduling because they’re worried I’ll miss something. That really bothers me, because I know I’m capable in the actual carpentry. I know the work. But these parts of the job make me look unreliable.\n\nAt home, my husband has had to take over the bills and reminders because if I’m the one handling papers, I’ll put them somewhere and then they’re just… gone. Or I’ll miss what needs to be done until it’s overdue or causing stress. He ends up carrying that mental load, and I hate that. So yes, overall, it’s affected my work, my money, my confidence, and my marriage. It’s not just an inconvenience.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]

At home, my husband has had to take over bills and reminders because I keep misplacing papers or missing what needs to be done.\n- key: duration\n  name: Duration\n  description: 1 year and 1 months\n  manifestation: This level of trouble has been going on for about a year and a month, since I broke my wrist and got shifted into shop paperwork and supervising instead of hands-on carpentry.\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 57 / carpenter / high_school\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S008\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Did you experience similar difficulties with focus and restlessness as a child, or did these issues start more recently?\n\nPatient: No, it didn’t start recently. It’s gotten more noticeable now, I think, because my work has more paperwork and estimates and things I have to sit and focus on, but when I really think back, the focus part was there when I was a kid.\n\nEven in grade school, before I was 12, my teachers were already telling my mother the same kinds of things. They’d say I was bright, I could understand the material, but I was careless. I remember that word because it felt like everybody thought I just wasn’t trying hard enough. I’d lose homework, or I’d forget what the instructions were supposed to be, or I’d start seatwork and then end up staring out the window while everyone else was finishing.\n\nSo, no, it doesn’t feel like this came out of nowhere later in life. Unfortunately, yes. I wish I could say it was just annoying to me, but it affects other people too.\n\nAt work it’s been a real problem. I’m a carpenter, and I’m supposed to be dependable with estimates, ordering materials, scheduling things, keeping track of what needs to happen next. And when I miss something or delay something, it doesn’t just stay on paper — it turns into somebody showing up and the materials aren’t there, or I forgot to order something, or I made a mistake and then we have to redo part of a job. That costs time, and sometimes it costs me commissions too, because if the estimate is late or wrong, or I don’t follow through fast enough, the work goes somewhere else or the job gets messed up.\n\nAnd I can tell the crew doesn’t rely on me the same way anymore for certain things. Nobody’s cruel about it, but I notice it. They’ll double-check me, or they’ll ask someone else about the schedule, or they don’t want me to be the one responsible for ordering because they’re afraid something will get missed. That feels awful, because I know my trade. I’m not lazy, and I’m not trying to dump work on anyone, but my mistakes make it look that way sometimes.\n\nAt home, it’s affected my husband too. He’s had to take over the bills and a lot of the reminders because if papers come into the house, I’ll set them somewhere “safe” and then they’re gone, or I’ll miss what needs to be done until it’s already a problem. He’ll remind me about things more than once, and I get embarrassed and defensive sometimes, even though he’s usually just trying to keep things from falling through the cracks.\n\nSo yes, it’s definitely caused strain. It’s not just in my head — other people end up having to work around me.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt like your difficulties with focus and restlessness have significantly impacted your overall quality of life?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean… very much so. If I’m late getting an estimate done, or I forget to order something, or I don’t keep the schedule straight, it can throw off the whole job. Or I’ll miss what needs to be done until it’s overdue or causing stress. He ends up carrying that mental load, and I hate that. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
It’s been there pretty much all along, I think. I mean, there may have been times when it didn’t stand out as much, depending on what was being expected of me, but when I look back, it wasn’t something that just appeared later.

Even back in grade school — before I was 12 — teachers were already saying things to my mother. And it was always that same kind of comment: that I was bright, that I understood things, but I was “careless.” I remember that word so clearly because it made me feel like they thought I just didn’t care. But I would lose homework, or I’d forget the instructions after they’d just been given, or I’d start doing seatwork and then somehow end up staring out the window while everyone else was finishing.

So, yes, it’s been consistent in that sense. It may have been easier to hide at certain times, or maybe people just saw it as me being scattered, but it was definitely there when I was young.

If I’m late getting an estimate done, or I forget to order something, or I don’t keep the schedule straight, it can throw off the whole job. Then materials aren’t there when they’re supposed to be, or something has to be redone, or we lose time trying to fix what should have been handled earlier. And that has cost me commissions, too. It’s not just embarrassing — it hits me financially.\n\nAnd I can feel that the crew doesn’t trust me the same way with certain responsibilities now. They’ll double-check me, or they’d rather someone else handle ordering or scheduling because they’re worried I’ll miss something. That really bothers me, because I know I’m capable in the actual carpentry. I know the work. But these parts of the job make me look unreliable.\n\nAt home, my husband has had to take over the bills and reminders because if I’m the one handling papers, I’ll put them somewhere and then they’re just… gone. Or I’ll miss what needs to be done until it’s overdue or causing stress. He ends up carrying that mental load, and I hate that. So yes, overall, it’s affected my work, my money, my confidence, and my marriage. It’s not just an inconvenience.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced difficulties with focus and restlessness consistently throughout your life, or have there been periods where these issues were less prominent?\n\nPatient: It’s been there pretty much all along, I think. I mean, there may have been times when it didn’t stand out as much, depending on what was being expected of me, but when I look back, it wasn’t something that just appeared later.\n\nEven back in grade school — before I was 12 — teachers were already saying things to my mother. And it was always that same kind of comment: that I was bright, that I understood things, but I was “careless.” I remember that word so clearly because it made me feel like they thought I just didn’t care. But I would lose homework, or I’d forget the instructions after they’d just been given, or I’d start doing seatwork and then somehow end up staring out the window while everyone else was finishing.\n\nSo, yes, it’s been consistent in that sense. It may have been easier to hide at certain times, or maybe people just saw it as me being scattered, but it was definitely there when I was young.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. I’ll want to stand up, walk across the shop, check something, move papers around, anything besides just sitting quietly and staying on that one task.\n\nMeetings are the same way.     "content": "/no_think\nYou are a patient experiencing Attention-Deficit/Hyperactivity Disorder (Predominantly Inattentive Presentation), in a diagnostic interview with a\npsychiatrist. Your goal is to answer the doctor's question naturally, using the\ninformation you are given.\n\nAnswer with the [Targeted Info], told in the way [Conversation Style] describes.\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Have you noticed your difficulties with focus and restlessness affecting your relationships with family, friends, or coworkers?\n\nPatient: Yes. Unfortunately, yes. I wish I could say it was just annoying to me, but it affects other people too.\n\nAt work it’s been a real problem. I’m a carpenter, and I’m supposed to be dependable with estimates, ordering materials, scheduling things, keeping track of what needs to happen next. And when I miss something or delay something, it doesn’t just stay on paper — it turns into somebody showing up and the materials aren’t there, or I forgot to order something, or I made a mistake and then we have to redo part of a job. That costs time, and sometimes it costs me commissions too, because if the estimate is late or wrong, or I don’t follow through fast enough, the work goes somewhere else or the job gets messed up.\n\nAnd I can tell the crew doesn’t rely on me the same way anymore for certain things. Nobody’s cruel about it, but I notice it. They’ll double-check me, or they’ll ask someone else about the schedule, or they don’t want me to be the one responsible for ordering because they’re afraid something will get missed. That feels awful, because I know my trade. I’m not lazy, and I’m not trying to dump work on anyone, but my mistakes make it look that way sometimes.\n\nAt home, it’s affected my husband too. He’s had to take over the bills and a lot of the reminders because if papers come into the house, I’ll set them somewhere “safe” and then they’re gone, or I’ll miss what needs to be done until it’s already a problem. He’ll remind me about things more than once, and I get embarrassed and defensive sometimes, even though he’s usually just trying to keep things from falling through the cracks.\n\nSo yes, it’s definitely caused strain. It’s not just in my head — other people end up having to work around me.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt like your difficulties with focus and restlessness have significantly impacted your overall quality of life?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean… very much so. It’s hard to say that without feeling like I’m making excuses, but yes, it has affected my life in a pretty serious way.\n\nAt work, the mistakes and delays aren’t small things anymore. If I’m late getting an estimate done, or I forget to order something, or I don’t keep the schedule straight, it can throw off the whole job. Then materials aren’t there when they’re supposed to be, or something has to be redone, or we lose time trying to fix what should have been handled earlier. And that has cost me commissions, too. It’s not just embarrassing — it hits me financially.\n\nAnd I can feel that the crew doesn’t trust me the same way with certain responsibilities now. They’ll double-check me, or they’d rather someone else handle ordering or scheduling because they’re worried I’ll miss something. That really bothers me, because I know I’m capable in the actual carpentry. I know the work. But these parts of the job make me look unreliable.\n\nAt home, my husband has had to take over the bills and reminders because if I’m the one handling papers, I’ll put them somewhere and then they’re just… gone. Or I’ll miss what needs to be done until it’s overdue or causing stress. He ends up carrying that mental load, and I hate that. So yes, overall, it’s affected my work, my money, my confidence, and my marriage. It’s not just an inconvenience.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced difficulties with focus and restlessness consistently throughout your life, or have there been periods where these issues were less prominent?\n\nPatient: It’s been there pretty much all along, I think. I mean, there may have been times when it didn’t stand out as much, depending on what was being expected of me, but when I look back, it wasn’t something that just appeared later.\n\nEven back in grade school — before I was 12 — teachers were already saying things to my mother. And it was always that same kind of comment: that I was bright, that I understood things, but I was “careless.” I remember that word so clearly because it made me feel like they thought I just didn’t care. But I would lose homework, or I’d forget the instructions after they’d just been given, or I’d start doing seatwork and then somehow end up staring out the window while everyone else was finishing.\n\nSo, yes, it’s been consistent in that sense. It may have been easier to hide at certain times, or maybe people just saw it as me being scattered, but it was definitely there when I was young.\n\n[Last Question]\nWere your difficulties with focus and restlessness noticeable to you or others during your teenage years?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 57 / carpenter / high_school\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m honestly not sure how noticeable it was then. I don’t have a clear answer for that period the way I do for when I was younger.

Teenage years are kind of blurry in my memory in terms of what other people saw or said. I can say I don’t remember it being talked about in any formal way, or anyone sitting me down and saying, “This is a problem.” But that doesn’t necessarily mean it wasn’t there — it may just mean I didn’t recognize it, or adults didn’t frame it that way.

So… I guess my answer is, I don’t really know. I don’t want to make it sound more certain than it is. I remember being scattered in general, but as far as whether it was clearly noticeable to me or to others during my teenage years, I can’t say for sure.

    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n16\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF90.0, F90.8, F90.9\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I’m here because I just… I can’t seem to keep my mind on one thing anymore, and it’s gotten to where it’s really messing up my days. I’m a carpenter, so I’m used to noise and people moving around, that’s not new, but lately it’s like one saw starting up, or somebody walking past the office, or even just me remembering some other job I’m supposed to check on, and I’m gone. My attention just gets pulled right off what I was doing.\n\nAnd then I’ll come back to it and realize I’ve lost a chunk of time, or I’m halfway into something else, and the whole day kind of slips away from me. It feels like I’m constantly being yanked from one thing to another, even when I’m trying hard to stay put mentally. That’s really what brought me in.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself fidgeting or moving around more than usual when you're trying to focus on a task?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. It’s not always like I’m tapping my foot every second or anything, but when I have to sit still and focus, especially at the desk doing estimates, I feel like I’m all wound up inside. Like my body is sitting there, but the rest of me is saying, get up, go check something, walk around, do anything else.\n\nLong meetings are bad for that too. I’ll be sitting there trying to listen and stay put, and I just get this uncomfortable need to move around. And the thing is, my wrist is healed now, so it’s not like I have some physical reason I can point to where I need to keep adjusting or getting up. I know I’m supposed to just sit there and finish what I’m doing, but it feels like I’m fighting myself the whole time.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking things through?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure I’d say that’s a big thing for me. I mean, everybody does something without thinking once in a while, I suppose, but I don’t think of myself as someone who’s constantly rushing into decisions or doing wild things on a whim.\n\nIf anything, I can get stuck turning something over in my head too much, especially if it’s important. So… no, I haven’t really noticed acting impulsively as one of the main problems. I’d have to think about what kinds of things you mean, though.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble keeping your workspace or tasks organized?\n\nPatient: Yes. That’s actually become one of the worst parts of it, especially since I’ve been stuck doing more of the shop paperwork. Out on a job site, things have a kind of order to them because the work is right in front of me, you know? But with paperwork, I’ll have bids that need finishing, schedules that need changing, invoices sitting there, and then an apprentice comes over with a question, and somehow it all just lands in one big pile on my bench.\n\nI’ll look at it and I honestly can’t always figure out what needs doing first. It’s not that I don’t care or I don’t know the work — I do — but deciding what to start with, what can wait, what has to go out today, it just gets tangled up. So instead of moving through it in order, I’ll start one thing, remember another thing, shift papers around, answer half a question, then suddenly I’m rushing at the end of the day and still missing deadlines. It’s embarrassing, because I feel like I should be able to keep bids, schedules, invoices, all of that straight, but lately it just piles up faster than I can sort it.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your mood lately?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’ve noticed a clear change in my mood, exactly. I mean, I get frustrated, sure, but I think anybody would when they’re trying to get things done and it feels like the day keeps slipping away from them. But as far as feeling like my mood has changed in some big way, I’m not sure I’d say that.\n\nI haven’t really been tracking it, if that makes sense. I don’t wake up and think, oh, I’m in a different mood than usual. It’s more like I notice how I’m doing based on how the day is going, and if things are backed up or I’m not keeping up, then I can get irritated with myself. But I don’t know if that counts as mood or just being aggravated by the situation.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself talking more than others in social situations?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I don’t think I’m usually the person taking over a whole conversation, if that’s what you mean. I mean, with people I know, I can talk plenty, especially if it’s about work or something practical, but I don’t think others would say I’m always talking more than everyone else.\n\nSometimes I might go on a bit if I’m trying to explain something, because I want to make sure I’m saying it right, and then I realize I’ve added more detail than maybe they needed. But socially, like at a gathering or with a group, I don’t see myself as especially talkative. If anything, I’m often listening or waiting for the right time to jump in. So… no, I haven’t really noticed that as a regular problem.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or feel the need to get up and move around when you're in quiet or low-stimulation environments?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. That’s actually one of the things that makes the desk work so hard for me. If I’m sitting there doing estimates, or if I’m stuck in a long meeting where everybody’s just talking through details, I start feeling really wound up inside. It’s not always like I’m jumping out of my chair right away, but inside I’m sort of crawling, like my body wants me to get up and move around.\n\nAnd the thing is, I know I’m supposed to just stay put and finish. My wrist is healed now, so it’s not like I have that excuse anymore where I’m limited physically or need to be careful in the same way. I’ll tell myself, “Just sit here, get through the estimates, finish this one thing,” but after a while it feels almost uncomfortable to keep sitting there. I’ll want to stand up, walk across the shop, check something, move papers around, anything besides just sitting quietly and staying on that one task.\n\nMeetings are the same way. If it’s short and to the point, I can manage better, but if it drags on, I feel like I’m holding myself in place. I’m trying to listen and look like I’m listening, but inside I’m thinking, I need to get up, I need to move, I need to do something with my hands. It’s frustrating because I’m not trying to be rude or impatient, but sitting still in that kind of quiet, slow situation feels a lot harder than it probably should.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find that unrelated thoughts keep popping into your mind when you're trying to focus?\n\nPatient: Yes. That’s probably one of the biggest reasons I’m here, honestly. I’ll be trying to do one thing—like sit down in the office and work through an estimate or figure out materials for a job—and it’s like my mind will not stay on that one track. It doesn’t take much at all.\n\nA saw starts up out in the shop, or somebody walks past the office door, or I hear a truck pull in, and suddenly my attention is gone. And sometimes it’s not even something outside me. I’ll just have a thought pop in about another job, like, “Oh, did we order that trim?” or “I need to call that customer back,” and then I’m off into that, and the thing I was doing is just sitting there half-finished.\n\nThe frustrating part is I don’t always notice right away. I’ll think I’m still working, but then I look up and I’ve spent twenty minutes bouncing between three different things, and the estimate I sat down to finish is still not done. Then the whole day starts getting away from me, because one interruption turns into another, and by the end I feel like I’ve been busy all day but didn’t actually keep my mind on what needed doing.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it difficult to dismiss thoughts that aren't relevant to what you're doing?\n\nPatient: Yes, very much. That’s really the part that wears me out, because once a thought gets in there, it’s not like I can just say, “That doesn’t matter right now,” and put it aside. I’ll be trying to keep my mind on one thing, like an estimate or some paperwork, and then one saw starts up, or somebody walks past the office, or I remember something from another job, and that’s it — I’m pulled off.\n\nAnd I’ll try to bring myself back, I really will. I’ll think, “No, finish what you’re doing first,” but the other thought keeps tugging at me. Like if I suddenly think, “Did we order that material?” then I feel like I have to check it right then, because otherwise I’m afraid I’ll forget. But then while I’m checking that, something else comes up, and before I know it I’m not doing the original thing at all.\n\nThat’s honestly what brings me in. I feel like I cannot keep my mind on one thing anymore. It’s not just being a little distracted here and there — it’s like the whole day can get away from me from one small thing pulling me off track.\n\nDoctor: Did you have a lot of energy or difficulty sitting still when you were a child?\n\nPatient: I don’t remember myself as being wild exactly, like running all over the classroom or anything, but yes, there were definitely problems even when I was little. Back in grade school, before I was 12, the comments my mother got from teachers were always kind of the same: that I was bright, but careless. That word came up a lot.\n\nThey’d say I could understand the work, but then I’d lose the homework, or forget what the instructions were, or I’d be sitting there staring out the window instead of finishing whatever seatwork we were supposed to be doing. I can still picture that, honestly — everybody else bent over their papers, and I’d be looking outside or thinking about something else, and then suddenly realize I hadn’t gotten much done.\n\nSo I don’t know if I’d describe it as “a lot of energy” so much as I had a hard time staying with what I was supposed to be doing while sitting still in class. It was enough that the teachers kept bringing it up to my mother.\n\nDoctor: Were you often described as fidgety or restless as a child?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I was described that way as a child, honestly. I don’t really remember teachers saying “fidgety” or “restless” specifically.\n\nWhat I can say is that now, if I have to sit at the desk doing estimates, or I’m stuck sitting through a long meeting, I get this wound-up feeling inside. It’s like my body is sitting there, but everything in me wants to get up and move around. And I know there’s no good reason for it — my wrist is healed, I’m physically able to just sit there and finish the work — but I feel this pressure, like I need to stand up, go check something, walk out to the shop, anything except keep sitting still.\n\nIt’s not always that I’m bouncing around in an obvious way. It’s more like I’m uncomfortable in my own skin when I’m forced to stay put. I’ll tell myself, “Just finish this estimate first,” but the longer I sit there, the more restless I feel.\n\nDoctor: Did you experience similar difficulties with focus and restlessness as a child, or did these issues start more recently?\n\nPatient: No, it didn’t start recently. It’s gotten more noticeable now, I think, because my work has more paperwork and estimates and things I have to sit and focus on, but when I really think back, the focus part was there when I was a kid.\n\nEven in grade school, before I was 12, my teachers were already telling my mother the same kinds of things. They’d say I was bright, I could understand the material, but I was careless. I remember that word because it felt like everybody thought I just wasn’t trying hard enough. I’d lose homework, or I’d forget what the instructions were supposed to be, or I’d start seatwork and then end up staring out the window while everyone else was finishing.\n\nSo, no, it doesn’t feel like this came out of nowhere later in life. The restlessness maybe I notice differently now, but the trouble staying focused and following through was there pretty early.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed your difficulties with focus and restlessness affecting your relationships with family, friends, or coworkers?\n\nPatient: Yes. Unfortunately, yes. I wish I could say it was just annoying to me, but it affects other people too.\n\nAt work it’s been a real problem. I’m a carpenter, and I’m supposed to be dependable with estimates, ordering materials, scheduling things, keeping track of what needs to happen next. And when I miss something or delay something, it doesn’t just stay on paper — it turns into somebody showing up and the materials aren’t there, or I forgot to order something, or I made a mistake and then we have to redo part of a job. That costs time, and sometimes it costs me commissions too, because if the estimate is late or wrong, or I don’t follow through fast enough, the work goes somewhere else or the job gets messed up.\n\nAnd I can tell the crew doesn’t rely on me the same way anymore for certain things. Nobody’s cruel about it, but I notice it. They’ll double-check me, or they’ll ask someone else about the schedule, or they don’t want me to be the one responsible for ordering because they’re afraid something will get missed. That feels awful, because I know my trade. I’m not lazy, and I’m not trying to dump work on anyone, but my mistakes make it look that way sometimes.\n\nAt home, it’s affected my husband too. He’s had to take over the bills and a lot of the reminders because if papers come into the house, I’ll set them somewhere “safe” and then they’re gone, or I’ll miss what needs to be done until it’s already a problem. He’ll remind me about things more than once, and I get embarrassed and defensive sometimes, even though he’s usually just trying to keep things from falling through the cracks.\n\nSo yes, it’s definitely caused strain. It’s not just in my head — other people end up having to work around me.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt like your difficulties with focus and restlessness have significantly impacted your overall quality of life?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean… very much so. It’s hard to say that without feeling like I’m making excuses, but yes, it has affected my life in a pretty serious way.\n\nAt work, the mistakes and delays aren’t small things anymore. If I’m late getting an estimate done, or I forget to order something, or I don’t keep the schedule straight, it can throw off the whole job. Then materials aren’t there when they’re supposed to be, or something has to be redone, or we lose time trying to fix what should have been handled earlier. And that has cost me commissions, too. It’s not just embarrassing — it hits me financially.\n\nAnd I can feel that the crew doesn’t trust me the same way with certain responsibilities now. They’ll double-check me, or they’d rather someone else handle ordering or scheduling because they’re worried I’ll miss something. That really bothers me, because I know I’m capable in the actual carpentry. I know the work. But these parts of the job make me look unreliable.\n\nAt home, my husband has had to take over the bills and reminders because if I’m the one handling papers, I’ll put them somewhere and then they’re just… gone. Or I’ll miss what needs to be done until it’s overdue or causing stress. He ends up carrying that mental load, and I hate that. It’s not just an inconvenience.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced difficulties with focus and restlessness consistently throughout your life, or have there been periods where these issues were less prominent?\n\nPatient: It’s been there pretty much all along, I think. And it was always that same kind of comment: that I was bright, that I understood things, but I was “careless.” I remember that word so clearly because it made me feel like they thought I just didn’t care.
